ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e267	No one has asked for the floor.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e281	The procedures for determining wages and salaries have for several years been added to a Commission, which sets out the salaries of the Grand Duchy, the Government and the Supreme Court according to what they think is right in relation to wage developments elsewhere in society. This way the Storting has done it for a long time, and SV has been critical of it, because there is one way in which the Storting is trying not to take political responsibility for the level of wages we have. We put it over to others, while we have to vote over it anyway. In practice, it is the Storting that has to adopt its wages; no one else can do so. For several years, SV has taken up proposals, as we have wanted a wider discussion of how the level of salary of the Storting should actually develop in relation to the rest of society. There may be different opinions about this, but SV believes it is important that our wages do not increase by a percentage, because then our wages in dollars will move from the average in the population. Great pay is good – they have to be. But there's no reason why the Storting should have a salary level that makes us live in a completely different reality than the average population that we're set to rule over and to be part of and facilitate so that it's possible for everyone with completely different financial assumptions than us to have a good life in this society. For a number of years, we have suggested that we do not have pay supplements. For example, during the financial crisis, there was no reason why the Storting should have increased wages when there was a great deal of uncertainty elsewhere. In normal years, we believe that the principle must be that we have a crown supplement, and that we calculate what is the average crown supplement for the other groups of society that have got their paychecks through. Of course, once we've calculated this, we've taken into account pay-rolling, overtime — all sorts of technical calculations — which means that we're going to land on a proper number. But the important thing about the principle and the proposal we are promoting today is that there is an annual crowning amount in the increase in average earnings in Norway. We discussed the agricultural situation a few days ago. Then several parties were very concerned that when you were going to close the pay gap, there was a raise in the crown. I also have a relatively long seagoing time in the trade movement. There we were very concerned that it was going to be additions, because percentages on a high number are a lot of money, while percentages on a low number are little money. Some people think it's equal to wage developments when social welfare recipients get 5 pst. pay increase like when we get it. I have a deep disagreement. No one goes to the store and trades with those percentages. That's the heads we can pay for our food, our rent and other expenses. That's the way it is for everyone. Therefore, it is most reasonable and correct that the Storting has an increase in earnings, which is determined on the basis of an increase in the annual crowning amount of an average salary in Norway. That is what we are proposing today. We also promote concrete suggestions about what it means in dollars and cents. We hope that the Storting will consider this seriously, because we believe that it should be a very correct and important signal to send to people that in terms of percentage calculation, which can be an advantage for each of us as individuals, as we get a little bit more money, we nevertheless adhere to the principles we believe are right, namely that it should be a crown supplement that is equal, and not that we through our way of calculating it gets more than others. This is also a proposal that meets those who think we should have a wage trend in line with others, because this is in line with others. In any case, those who feel close to the left side of Norwegian policy and the trade union movement should seriously consider this, because it is traditionally the way you have thought when negotiating wages, not to increase the differences between groups, but, on the contrary, to try to reduce them over time. Of course, it is also the SV's political objective that we should have less economic differences. Then it is important that the Storting has a payroll system that is in line with it and that does not increase the salaries of the Storting more than the average of the other gets in their payroll.

There was a reason that the scheme once existed. The pay discussion in the United Kingdom became difficult. It also became difficult in relation to the public because it set its own salaries without having a kind of corrective body that was invited to have an opinion on the level of pay. And it's not like we're depriving ourselves of responsibility for the decision, we're actually making the decision, and at this moment we have a debate about it. I think that's important to emphasize. So what we get from the payroll commission is a piece of advice that we can choose to follow or not follow. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e438	In the proposal for a representative we are now discussing, the representative and the prime minister, Olemic Thommess, are proposing to establish a new national human rights institution that is being organised under the Stortinget and linked to the Civil Ombudsman. The Presidency agrees that it is a good solution for the National Institute for Human Rights to be reestablished as a body governed by the Storting. The Presidency further believes that the Stortinget has a responsibility to contribute to ensuring that human rights are upheld in Norway. We do that both by agreeing to the ratification of Norway's international obligations and by ensuring that the government follows up the human rights obligations of society towards its citizens. The size of the population has just adopted constitutional changes aimed at strengthening the place of human rights in society. The Presidency believes that an active and outspoken national institution for human rights can be an important instrument both to contribute to increased awareness of human rights in general and to focus on human rights issues in public vocabulary. The Presidency believes that it is in line with the role and responsibility of the Grand Duchy to commit itself to setting up a new national institution for human rights. The organisation of the institution during the Storting process will also contribute to the institution’s prominent status as such. The Presidency departs from the draft representative on an essential point. This concerns the link with the Civil Ombudsman. The Presidency has received a number of recordings, such as: pointed to less fortunate experience in the fact that the Centre for Human Rights has also acted as a national institution, and the recommendations given to the Presidency by several teams have been to establish an independent institution with its own identity without formal links with the Civil Ombudsman. These recommendations follow the presidency of the current suspension. The Presidency has stressed the independence and independence of national institutions. This is a central element of the UN principles for national institutions, called the Paris principles. In the nomination, the Presidency advocates the establishment of a new national human rights institution under the auspices of the Stortinge — in other words, without formal links with the Civil Ombudsman. Let me make it clear that Vice-President Kenneth Svendsen had primarily wanted to place a national institution directly under the Civil Ombudsman, as an extension of the Ombudsman's human rights mandate as a national prevention mechanism. However, the representative, Mr Svendsen, substantially supports the proposal in today's vote. The independent and independent position of the national institution is underlined by the fact that the head of the institution is elected by the Stortinget and is given an independent responsibility for its professional activities, by the fact that the institution is to report directly to the Stortinget in the form of an annual notification, and that the allocation to the national institution is to be shown in a separate chapter of the State budget, in line with the other external institutions of the Stortinget. However, the Presidency believes that there are good reasons for an administrative link and co-location with the Civil Ombudsman, such as: to use some common administrative services. The Presidency assumes that the mandate and tasks of the national institution shall be in accordance with the framework for which the Paris principles provide instructions. This will be a prerequisite for the International Coordination Committee for National Institutions (ICC) to be able to recognise the institution as a fully-fledged national institution and to award it A status, which is an internationally recognised quality stamp. This gives important rights in the context of international human rights and will strengthen Norway's reputation and role as a credible player in the field of human rights internationally. According to the Paris principles, it is essential that the institution be given a broad mandate to promote and strengthen human rights in accordance with the national and international legal standards that we have committed ourselves to. In the run-up, the presidency has highlighted several core tasks for national institutions, such as monitoring the human rights situation in our own country and conducting investigations, in particular in societal areas facing human rights challenges. Where necessary, the institution shall be a driver for action. The institution shall provide professional advice on human rights issues to both public authorities and private actors and shall be open to requests from individuals. The Presidency considers that the institution should not deal with complaints, but of course it will be able to refer to relevant bodies, such as existing ombudsmen or the judiciary. It is also stressed that national institutions must have their main responsibilities in national matters and that they must have sufficient weight and competence in their field of competence. The Presidency shall promote in its nomination a proposal that the Storting should empower the Presidency to draw up legislative proposals and instructions for the national institution. In this context, it is important to establish a rapid selection of trade unions. The mandate and tasks of the national institution will be further explored in this context. The Presidency considers that the Storting should be given the opportunity to adopt laws and instructions during the harvest session, so that a new national institution can be established as from 1 January 2015. Finally, I would like to mention that the Presidency believes that it should be legislated that the Stortinge should appoint an advisory board in relation to a national institution. This will include, inter alia: represent non-governmental organisations, the ombudsmen, the Sameting and the academic community. The Committee will have an important role as an advisory body for the activities of the institution and could contribute to the coordination of the efforts of different human rights actors. In particular, the Advisory Forum will reflect the diversity to be dealt with and maintained by the national institution. I recommend the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e625	The size currently deals with a proposition where the government asks the Stortinget to be allowed to implement step 3 in the development of the Joint Strike Missile, JSM. Step 3 will prepare the missile for production and integrate it on Norway’s new fighter aircraft, F-35. Producer of JSM is Kongsberg Defence & Aerospace, KDA. The development of the missile has KDA done in cooperation with the Defence, Defence Agency, FFI, and of course also the manufacturer of F-35, Lockheed Martin. JSM will, on successful development, become the superior best missile in its class in the future. JSM is a long-range anti-surface weapon. JSM is a further development of Norwegian sea target missile, NSM, which is the world's most advanced class. NSM has a range of 200 km and the battlehead weighs 125 kg. The JSM range is 280 km. The battlehead weighs 120 kg. The KDA has managed to export NSM, and the price of a missile is between 15 million. NOK 20 million. kr. Of course, it is Norway’s ambition that KDA also achieves exports of JSM. JSM is a stop to evolution after 50 years of missile development on Kongsberg. JSM is full of computing power and electronics. The missile processes optical data and makes its own choices in line with the mission's purpose. The missile is probably more intelligent than most in this room. It has been an ambition to turn the further development and integration of JSM into multinational cooperation. The size has previously decided that international cooperation must be a prerequisite for the further development of the missile. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e704	Events in recent years in Libya and now in Eastern Europe have once again underlined the importance of a better burden-sharing within NATO. Norway wants to take part in the security responsibilities of the alliance through further development and strengthening of its defence. JSM is a long-range and precision-driven anti-surface missile developed through a partnership between Kongsberg Defence & Aerospace and the Defence. In many ways the missile acts as a small self-guided aircraft, which can solve a specific mission and give the Defense an ability to take out, as I said, well-defenced targets at a long distance. The main objectives will be modern marine vessels with advanced self-defence systems, but JSM can also be used against landmines. In order to carry out these tasks, JSM must be able to achieve the right objective, avoid the defence systems of the target and take exactly where it is intended. This results in JSM through a unique combination of technical solutions. Several of these have been developed from scratch in Norway. The missile is also the first of its kind specifically designed to fit the inside of the weapons room on F-35. This allows F-35 to carry up to two JSMs inside — one missile in each weapons room. The fighting light thus retains its low radar signature and remains difficult to detect for the opponent. No other known missiles, either in production or in development, have this combination of technical solutions, and JSM will therefore collectively offer a unique operational capability for both Norway and other users of F-35. Much of this comes through the delivery of parts and technology for the production of F-35, but even more will come as the delivery of weapons and in the form of support for the planes throughout their lifetime. JSM is important for industrial cooperation and therefore the possibility of selling missiles to others is significant. By comparison, Kongsberg has sold over 1,000 Penguin-type marine missiles to a number of different countries for use on both ships, helicopters and aircraft since it was introduced in the early 1970s. Development of defence technology is expensive and JSM is no exception. Not only is technology very complex – it is quite unique, so much needs to be designed from scratch. The development of JSM has until now been implemented within the cost framework set by Stortinget. An independent evaluation of the technical design of JSM conducted in winter 20l3 to 20l4 has confirmed that JSM has the technological maturity as foreseen at that stage. A significant part of the development of JSM has been implemented and the basic design has been completed. The missile is technically sufficiently mature according to the requirements of the start-up of the integration of F-35. The development of JSM is therefore considered to be on track, with a view to achieving full operational capability for Norwegian F-35 in 2025. JSM is, as many have been involved, a further development of Naval Strike Missile, NSM, which was developed over the 1990s in cooperation between the Defence and the KDA. Further development of the NSM to JSM, for use on new fighter aircraft, increases Norway’s ability to exert long-range influence and provides the Defence Force with the ability to combat over-distance landings. The government today asks for the Storting’s approval to implement step 3 of the JSM development cycle. The development of JSM then takes place in three stages, of which steps 1 and 2 have been implemented. This proposition deals with step 3 and the elements of the qualification and integration to be contracted with KDA. This applies to the production of test missiles and related KDA engineering support. The defence is primarily responsible for facilitating the qualification and integration of the missile. The qualification shall certify that the weapon can be delivered from a fighter aeroplane, in accordance with the given requirements. This is an area where US testing and development environments have particular expertise, and this service will therefore be acquired through US authorities. The integration of the missile is the work on the aircraft itself, and in particular the aircraft's software. The integration itself will be part of the first upgrade of F-35 after the development phase has been completed. This first upgrade of the aeroplane is known as Block 4, and this upgrade shall, on schedule, be fully operational for 2022 to 2024. The acquisition of the missile by the defence is carried out as part of the fighter aircraft programme once the development has been completed and the missile is integrated into the F-35. The Stoltenberg II government considered it a realistic goal to establish multi-national cooperation for the integration of JSM after JSM development stage 2. Norway has received sufficient support from the multinational partnership to ensure that JSM will be integrated already into the Block 4 upgrade of the aeroplane. In addition, there is a strong interest in JSM from the partner countries Australia, Canada and, in part, Italy and the United States. Japan has also shown great interest in JSM. I am pleased that today I can present completely new information to the Stortinget that a partnership related to the integration of JSM on F-35 is now planned. Australia has signaled that they want cooperation related to the integration and testing of JSM on F-35. Furthermore, they wish to look at the possibility of contributing to the development of missile competence and industrial involvement. Through a dialogue over the next six to twelve months, we will have clarified the content of a formal agreement. In the implementation of JSM development stage 3, Norwegian industry participation will be significantly extended. The KDA has estimated that they will involve 60 Norwegian companies as subcontractors in the project. This includes multi-area component suppliers, including mechanics, electronics, wiring, explosives and telemetry. In addition, several enterprises are involved in other technical deliveries. The KDA itself has estimated that JSM will have a significant industrial impact and has estimated the potential for national value creation up to 20-25 billion. kr. JSM development stages 1 and 2 have been implemented within the framework and assumptions previously informed by the Stortingen and taken into account for its decisions. Steps 1 and 2 have given both the KDA and the Defence a more complete picture of the remaining challenges related to the development and integration of the missile. As a result, several areas have been identified within the last part of the development where more work is needed than previously foreseen. As a result, together with several technical changes, the cost of the last part of the development will be higher than that previously foreseen. Overall, the cost of the third and final phase of development is 1.05 billion. kr higher than the calculation made in 2012. In the 2012 cost calculation, it was considered that the integration costs for JSM were to be shared equally with two other partner nations. For the time being, it has to be considered that Norway has to bear the total integration costs alone. This therefore implies increased costs for Norway, although the price of integration itself has not increased. Compared to the cost calculations made in 2012, the integration cost will be 1.15 billion. kr higher than the calculations made in 2012. Overall, the overall result is that the development, qualification, integration and acquisition of JSM totals 2.2 billion. kr to the right of the previous estimate. In total, this increases the expected cost of the fighter aircraft programme from 64 billion to 66.2 billion. kr. There has been a long debate with the KDA, it started last August. From the Ministry of Defence's side, we have been concerned about getting the best possible solution, and it is also reflected in the setting which is the main elements of the agreement that has now been negotiated. Nevertheless, I would like to stress that, on the part of the Ministry, we are now also launching an external assistance review to identify what is causing the programme to become more expensive. In the proposal, the government proposes to cover the additional cost of 200 million. kr in 2014 as an additional appropriation for the defence budget, i.e. outside the current defence framework. The government, as in steps 1 and 2, will return to the Storting in the usual way, through the ordinary budgetary processes, recommending the recovery of the remaining part of the increase in costs. I am pleased that the Committee joins in the implementation of the JSM stage 3. It is absolutely central to ensuring that Norway will be able to achieve full operational capability on the new fighter aircraft in 2025. The combination of F-35 and JSM will significantly strengthen our operational capability and not least strengthen the war-preventing threshold. It will also further consolidate the role of KDA as a missile supplier and as a technological spearhead. In conclusion, I would like to make a few comments on what many have been concerned about, namely industrial return and industrial agreements linked to the programme. The lifetime industrial return objective up to 2054 is at the base of 40 billion. kr, which is the purchase price of the aeroplanes themselves. There are in particular three things that will contribute: JSM is in a separate class with regard to the possibility of industrial returns, but we also have APEX ammunition and composites as important, large areas. Agreements have now been concluded for approx. 2.2 B.C.E. kr. It's not too high a number, and we also have clear expectations and clearly express to Lockheed Martin and the US authorities what expectations we have. That's what I do when I travel to the United States, or have a meeting here with the fighter aircraft program in the United States, our fighter aircraft director, does the department, and so does the Stortinget in their meetings with Lockheed Martin. And then we're going to continue with our impatience. We're going to keep pressing, and we're very clear that this is as clearly expressed from the Storing as it can be. At the same time, we should also remember that right now the production of aeroplanes is relatively low – entirely according to the plan. When that production increases — including that under the plan — it is also more likely that the volume on the Norwegian side will increase as long as we are able to keep the Norwegian defence industry competitive so that they can deliver on the best value principle, which is the basis of acquisitions.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e904	According to the Constitution § 75f, the Stortinget is “to submit to the minutes of the Council of State and all public reports and papers;” After Stortinget's Rules of Procedure § 14 nr. 9 the Control and Constitutional Committee shall "examine and make an adjustment to the size of: (a) Protocols of the Council of State, etc., as set out in: Basic law § 75(f). Fredrik Sejersted writes in his legal dissertation ‘Control and Constitution’ that when the control regime of the Storting was established in 1814, control of the State Council protocols was the cornerstone itself. The scheme was incorporated into the Basic Law § 75, according to the Swedish model. During the relatively strict distribution of power, it was in principle important that the Storting thus had a constitutional requirement of transparency in the central body of the executive. In addition to the 1800s, protocol control also played a real political role, both as an information resource and as a starting point for dealing with individual issues. Until well into the middle-war period, the annual adjustments to the State Council Protocols were often very extensive, and the debates were used to settle with the government in some individual cases. Later it has gradually gone downwards. Formally, the system is preserved, but in reality, this control has almost completely played its part. The attitudes are brief, and there is usually no debate. This may be due, inter alia, to: that the Government is now controlled by the Storting by other means, so that there is no longer any need for the annual settlement of the previous protocol debates. The Committee has no comment on the minutes of the Council of State for the second half of 2013, either in respect of admissibility cases, appointments of office or laid down, amended or repealed rules. In its opinion, the Committee states that it ‘will further discuss how the regulatory review can best be managed within the available investigation capacity and competences’. Previous committees have also regularly discussed the possibility of making more use of the ordinary protocol control. There has been a desire to use the protocols as a starting point for the ongoing control of government regulations to see if they have been maintained within the limits of the powers and assumptions of the Storting. The problem — and this is important — has been to achieve something more than mere formal control. In a modern regulatory country, the size depends on being able to delegate legislative powers to the administration, but no ongoing consultation procedures have been established. However, the size may follow-up control of the government’s regulations. The Frøiland Committee assessed whether more real and systematic procedures should be put in place for the parliamentary control of regulations, but did not propose concrete proposals on how to carry out regulatory control. It was assumed that the control committee itself was closest to evaluating this. That is why my message today is also directed at the presidency. The challenge is that one should come back to this at a later stage in what is called an appropriate form. It will be absolutely necessary for us in Stortinget to discuss our ability to carry out proper and precise regulatory control, as required by law.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e932	First, I would like to agree with the committee chairman's comments on this matter. At the same time, I would like to add a few comments to the regulatory review. As we all know, regulations are important. In a number of specific areas, it is the regulations that demonstrate the practical application of legislation. My responsibility concerns regulations under the responsibility of the Ministry of the Environment, and I have some comments on this. While all the regulations of the Ministry of the Environment contained in the present document are governed by the Nature Diversity Act, four regulations are laid down in the Marka Act § 11. These are the regulations for the protection of Schännungsåsen, Godbecken, Hauktjern and Spinneren open-air areas in the municipality of Oslo. I would just like to stress that Section 37 of the Natural Diversity Act, on nature reserves, is considered to be the basis for the protection of areas with special qualities due to natural experience values, but it has a different protection purpose than the Marka Act § 11, precisely because the Marka Act § 11 is not intended to provide professional, biological qualities. The requirements of the four above-mentioned open-air areas are therefore properly provided for in Section 11 of the Marka Act. Privately owned areas of regional or national protection value are open to protection by means of a voluntary protection process, under the Nature Diversity Act § 37. But this is to be done according to professional biological qualities, and not because of natural experience values. That's where we're at a very fundamental distinction. Paragraph 11 of the Marka Act does not, therefore, apply to professional biological qualities, but to natural experience values of special quality. In order to protect under Section 37 of the Nature Diversity Act, it is the professional, biological qualities that must be taken into account.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e943	I would like to support the rapporteur's comments. We have a somewhat complicated administration, and the regulatory diversity – or what I'm going to call it – is incredibly big, and different governments use it differently. Today's government is signaling a lot of activity on the prescription page. They have every right to do so, but the Storting and the Control and Constitutional Committee are responsible for ensuring that this is in line with the law we have, and what the Storting has adopted. It's a very, very comprehensive process. The representative Lundtägen was up and explained one example where one has to weigh, look and then find out what law it is actually based in, and see if it should have been based in another law, etc. It's very complicated, and I don't think I'm taking my mouth too full by saying that this is practically not feasible with the resources we have available today. That's why I'm also very happy that the committee's chairman says that you're going to bring this back to the presidency in an appropriate form. We are obliged to say that we have checked all these regulations, but in practice – I think I should be so honest to say – it is simply not possible to do so. I think most of us feel a pretty big discomfort by saying that we've done it, when it's actually not possible to do it in practice. I think we had to extend the session quite significantly and staff the secretariat with quite a number of posts if we were to go through it that way. I would urge the presidency – when it gets this enquiry – to take this seriously, and that we go through it, so that the Storting is actually able to control and take responsibility for the checks we carry out, that we are doing it in a way that is in line with the obligations we have to be imposed at all times, and that we have the resources to do it properly.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e979	The case concerns the price control in the concession law. The background is a letter from the Council of State Listhaug of 4 December 2013 to municipalities, county councillors and State Agriculture Administration, where I will quote the last sentence: ‘The Ministry therefore recommends that the price agreed by the parties is not given decisive importance in the assessment of whether a concession should be granted.’ On this basis, there has been an exchange of letters later between the Control Committee, the Prime Minister and Minister of Agriculture Listhaug. The last meeting of the Committee asked whether it was in accordance with the Rules of Procedure to consider the matter in the Control Committee when the matter was recently discussed in the Business Committee, Document 8:29 S of this year. Questions were asked whether the proposal for a decision should be rejected as a result of a provision of Section 38 of the Rules of Procedure. The Constitutional Department here in Stortingen has decided that the proposals are different enough for this to be no problem. So the foundation is present for treatment in Stortinget today. What's shown in the setting is that we as parties don't agree on anything. The progress party and the Right have their own comments from the first sentence, and I expect that the sails will justify their vision. All parties, other than the Party of Advancement and the Right, point out that the law on the concession for the acquisition of immovable property, the law on the concession of 28 November 2003, is the end of a development introduced by the Civil Code of 1888. The concession rules have been policy tools in the governance of society throughout this period. In Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1 in today's concession law, on specific conditions for agricultural properties, states that: ‘In deciding on the application for a concession for the acquisition of property to be used for agricultural purposes, particular attention shall be paid to: 1. whether the agreed price corresponds to socially sound price developments’. The majority point to the fact that according to the comments of Gyldendal Rättdata on the concession law, note 76, last revised on 12 June 2012, states that: ‘The moments referred to in the first subparagraph may be weighted more than those referred to in the second and third subparagraphs’. In addition, note 77 states: ‘The conformity of the agreed price with socially sound price developments shall be assessed. Otherwise, the concession application may be rejected. With socially sound price developments, it is necessary, inter alia, to: is understood as a price which contributes to the achievement of the objectives of agricultural policy. This does not apply if the property is acquired for non-agricultural purposes and it is not foreseeable that the property will be maintained as agricultural property in the future. Where it is objectively foreseeable that agricultural holdings are eligible for production subsidies, price controls shall be carried out’. And on the other hand, ‘The starting point for the price evaluation is the yield value of the productive land and the re-encapsulation value of the buildings. These include: indicated a minimum value for agricultural land in different areas of the country and a framework for adding housing value; in circular M-1/2010, the upper framework for adding housing value is now set at NOK 1500000. Additional soils may be provided with a supplement of up to 50 pst. A capitalisation rate of 4 pst has been established. Circular M-2/2012 provides that if the price of a built-up property is less than NOK 2,50000, price evaluation shall be omitted.’;» And finally, about the regulation: ‘I ‘Procedure for the examination of proceedings, etc. in the municipality in cases under the Concessions Act, the Soil Law, the Odelsgesetz and the Forestry Act’ laid down by the LMD on 8 December 2003, the amendment of 23 June 2009 in the second paragraph of Section 1 provides that in cases relating to the application for the concession of property to be used for agricultural purposes, the decision shall show how the price assessment has been made in relation to the individual parts of the property. [[no 1480]] [[no 831]] Where, on a discretionary basis, a price different from that at which the valuation of the individual parts is based is obtained, the main considerations which have been decisive for that purpose shall be stated.’;» This is part of a complete record of Gyldendal Corrective Data on how to understand the provisions. Therefore, the claim of the Right and Advance Party in the comment on the reference to the Golden Valley Straight Data – that those comments are not complete – is incorrect. It's a positive error. Your claim that the record from the current law gives a distorted picture of the content of the law is therefore also incorrect. The majority of the Committee points out that in its letter of 4 December 2013 of the Ministry of Agriculture and Food to the municipalities, the counties and the State Agriculture Administration, entitled ‘Practisation of price controls’, among others. is: ‘Price control may have adverse effects on agriculture, including: because it can dampen the desire for investment of the owners.’» Further: «A viable and dynamic agriculture depends on a well-functioning market for the marketing of agricultural property.» Further: ‘The government wants to increase the turnover of agricultural properties and has the ambition to review legislation with a view to simplification. However, this may take some time.» End: ‘On this basis, the Ministry considers that price control practices should facilitate increased turnover.’ And then the decisive sentence comes: ‘The Ministry therefore recommends that the price agreed by the parties is not given decisive importance in the assessment of whether a concession should be granted.’ There is a clear understanding in the Committee that the content of the circulars on price assessments has changed over time. There has been a lot of discussion about the content, but at all times these circulars have laid down the basis for the price of concessional property, whether it was within a socially sound price development. When it has been so central, it is seaworthy because in agriculture and agriculture income policy targets are defined. It is income policy objectives for agriculture and income policy objectives are not linked to return on capital. The objective is linked to the work payment to be made by the work well done. Thus, the context is that if prices are too high, the remainder remaining for a seafarer for labour income will be lower. Therefore, there is a clear link between the general economic policy of agricultural policy and the law to ensure that the income targets are met. That's why we have the price control in the concession law. What has now happened is that at the letter of the department of 4.12.13 the price control is again just like a shell, without content. The real content is being removed, and that's why this case is where we are, because it's a breach of the constitutional basis for the ministry's work. At the same time, on the day that the Ministry sent out this letter, they also sent out the ‘Proposal to repeal the provisions on ‘price control’ in the concession law’. The Ministry then wrote in its letter of consultation: ‘The Ministry of Agriculture and Food proposes to repeal paragraph 9 of the first paragraph. 1 of the concession law of 28 November 2003 No 98.’» On this basis, a majority of the Committee shall depart from this. And the members of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the Socialist Left will point out that there is a safe administrative tradition that the Ministry of Agriculture and Food through circulars has clarified the principles underlying price control in the concession law. This has not previously been done through recommendations from the Ministry. By letter of the Ministry of Agriculture and Food of 4 December 2013, the previous circular describing the principles of price control was effectively terminated. Furthermore, the members of the Committee of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party say that the Ministry's letter of 4 December 2013 prejudges the Stortinget's consideration of the government's consultation proposal to abolish price control in the concession law. This majority believe that it is critical that the government sent letters to municipalities, county councillors and the State's agricultural administration to in effect disregard Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1 in the treatment of concession, because the government proposes to cancel it later. What is more, all parties, including the Left and the Christian People's Party, point out that the hearing on the abolition of price controls ended on 1 February 2014. The hearing opinions show that the view of the price control was shared. The same majority believe that there are good reasons to look at the regulation of price control in the concession law, but look at it, look at the principles that exist and follow the secure administrative tradition that has been, namely to have circulars defining the content of price control. On this basis, the same majority say that it was unfair for the government to send letters to municipalities, statesmen and the SLF to in effect disregard Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1 in the treatment of concession, because the government will propose to cancel this. The letter from the government presupposes that Storting's legal handling of the government's announced proposal to abolish price control in the concession law. Finally, on this basis, the Labour Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party promote the following proposals: ‘The Commission asks that a letter entitled ‘Practice of the price control of 4 December 2013 to the municipalities, the county councillors and the state agricultural management to disregard Section 9 of the First paragraph of the concession law. 1 immediately be withdrawn.’;» I recommend the Committee's position.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e1120	It has been interesting — as a phenomenon — to hear the first two posts. First, we heard the case rapporteur, who is one of the largest followers of the regulatory economy, and I do not overlook the fact that the underlying attitude towards regulating – in this case the price of agricultural property – may have infected the legal outlay we were given. Despite the immediate agreement of the head of the control committee, I would nevertheless prefer to say that there are some faulty premises in the argument here. We are still insisting that we have not been able to reproduce the current law either in the notes or at the layout that has arrived here today. It is simply due to the fact that the law itself provides an indication that there are five aspects to which particular emphasis can be placed under the first paragraph of Section 9 of the Concession Act. The decision as to whether to authorise the transfer of property will therefore depend on a test as to whether these five considerations are present — whether they are present in the individual case and with what weight they are present in the case on the table. Then you make a decision after an overall assessment. These considerations are therefore, as has been pointed out, the desire for socially sound price developments, consideration of the location in the area, operational conditions, suitability for the acquirer, consideration of generally sound resource management, for example. the protection of the cultural landscape, and there are other elements in the second and third paragraphs which can also be weighted. When the legislator, i.e. the Storting, has previously used the term ‘among other things’, it is a warning that the considerations mentioned are to be properly taken into account and assessed. But that doesn't mean that there's been an exhaustive calculation once and for all of the considerations that can be attributed to weight. Thus, other elements can be considered when they are relevant in order to fulfil the purpose of the concession law. It has been a practice for differing ministers of agriculture and food to send circulars to assist in the uniform administration of the law of the concession authorities. Among other things, a 2009 circular states: ‘Concession authorities must decide on the extent to which the different considerations apply, i.e.: whether they are relevant in the case and with what weight they act in the case. Special attention shall be paid to the law, with a high degree of emphasis. However, in an overall assessment where such considerations are to be assessed in relation to other relevant elements, it is not always that such elements have a decisive impact on the outcome of the case. There may be various reasons for this, such as the fact that the other elements together draw towards another solution, or that the consideration which is to be given special attention under the law does not apply with particular strength in the specific case. An example is the acquisition of properties in areas where the purpose of residence is not, or only to a small extent, applied. In such cases, less attention may be given to this consideration in the circumstances than to other considerations relevant under the law.’;» The interesting thing is that this I am now quoting is a circular drawn up – one can almost doubt whether that is true – by a party trap by the case rapporteur, Lundtägen. It has been developed by former Minister of Agriculture Lars Peder Breck. The assessment made in the circular is in accordance with the legislative will expressed in this Chamber. This assessment can be found in the preparatory work and is based on practical decisions, including: a decision in the Ministry from 17 October 2008, which states: ‘The system of law, however, is not such that a concession application should be rejected without further delay even if the price is too high on the basis of the guidelines. There is an opportunity to take into account other relevant elements which fall within the scope of the law.’;» This shows with all due clarity that when a new circular under the current Council of State states states that pristine reasons are not necessarily to be given decisive weight, a tradition from the past is included. And ‘decisive’ means that when the price is on one side and the other calculated considerations are on the other, it is not that the price question alone should lead to the transferee being rejected. It is also interesting that regulators take it for granted that regulated prices should be low in order to take account of societal developments. I want to stay at that reasoning a little bit. Thus, the term ‘socially sound price’ refers almost to the synonymous meaning that there should be artificially regulated underprices at the same time. We have gained quite animal-bought experience with price regulation that has worked over time in the agricultural sector, but not least outside. What's our experience is that price regulation strips the owners of the outback by owning property, while the owner has to bear the full downside risk. In addition to the sales situation, which the concession law aims to regulate, such regulation also reduces — i.e. pressuring people to convert to underprice — the suitability of the property as collateral for loans, it undermines the foundation of agricultural mortgages. I heard an economic comment from the case rapporteur, but if we first address the social impact, we must also look at the effects of the fact that owners of such priced property also have to bear higher financing costs for investment, typically by extension, by modernisation or by extensive maintenance taken at regular intervals. Thus, it can simply be argued that the objective of the law on socially sound pricing is precisely to emphasise the realistic market price, which is at the same time the most socially sound one. This could be achieved without changing the law, by reference to the purpose clause of the law, through dynamic interpretation of the law, the interpretation of the law to which the rigid wording of the law is to be adapted to a new reality and new experience. Now the government has announced that you want a review, you want an updated law. Of course, what is then the evaluation theme of the control committee is not the regulatory approach or the view of agricultural policy, it is whether the letter of 4 December 2013 from the Minister of Agriculture and Food respects the law or is on the outside of the law we have. Then it's perfectly obvious, with what I've shown, that you've also explained in the past in the same street and from the same reasoning that the current Council of State has done, that you've been loyal within the limits of the current law. In this picture, it is very timely that the Council of State has given the concession authorities a reminder of the degrees of freedom that exist within the law as it is today, without foreshadowing – of course – the law that comes. I would also like to say that if you quote several parts of the letter, instead of characterizing it quite unfairly as setting aside the law – zeroing price regulation – you will see from a concrete review of the letter that has been criticised here by the case rapporteur and the chairman of the control committee that the letter itself shows that there is still a price control, while recommending to the municipalities that they make the overall assessment that has also been based on previous circulars. The law no longer lays down the decisive emphasis on price when assessing whether to grant a concession, and this is – as I have mentioned – in the preparatory work of the law, where it is demonstrated that the discretionary assessments to be made can be adapted to changes in the development of society and changing policy considerations. Thus, the provision is designed to enable it to take account of the fact that the view of what is socially sound price developments can change over time. In relation to the purpose of the law, Ot.prp. No 79 for 2002–03 stated that the law could take account of changing assessments. The proposition reads as follows: ‘The word is so liberal that it allows for development and change of an actual nature in technical, economic, social and other areas, including: with regard to needs and resources. It provides, among other things: playroom for changing perceptions and reassessments.’» What we see in this case is that it is this room of action that has been used, loyally within the current law, when the letter was sent out to the municipalities, the county councillors and the state agricultural administration. It is not a space for action that the Council of State has taken, but a space for action, the Storting knowingly and willfully opened up in the 2003 Act. So, of course, you can ask: does there any independent purpose serve to remind you of the space of action in a law that you have notified formal changes to if not for long? I want to say that the answer to that is also an obvious yes. But it's not for the sake of the political sphere, it's for the sake of the users of the law today. The Ministry's letter of 4 December was sent to avoid creating uncertainty in municipalities and among sellers and buyers about how the practice of price control should take place until Stortinget has made the clarifications in the law that one wants. The letter therefore intends to contribute to the treatment of applicants by different municipalities in this intermediate phase, which is in the same situation, which is also a foundation of good administration. And once again, the letter goes into an undisputed practice where it has just been up to the serving Council of State to define more precisely what is being stressed within the boundaries of the law. It is therefore of course not in any way critical that the government sent out the letter in question. The reason why the letter was sent out was precisely the desire to avoid uncertainty in the market for the marketing of agricultural property. Such uncertainty could also have as a social unfortunate consequence that turnover would stop pending the handling of the law by the Storting. Consequently, there is no cause for Stortinget to ask the Ministry to take steps to recall the letter. I see the attempts made on this occasion, which – let us say – are more political in the context of an underlying reality of agricultural policy, which is not really within the remit of the Control Committee.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e1172	The Progress Party believes that this should not be a matter for the Stortinget, and has in the past also completed that topic in the Committee. I see the concession law as a flexible law, which is designed to continuously evaluate different social conditions. Already in Chapter 1, Section 1, which deals with objectives and instruments, it is said: ‘The purpose of the law is to regulate and control the movement of real estate in order to achieve effective protection of agricultural production areas and of the most beneficial ownership and use conditions for society, inter alia. to meet: the needs of future generations. the agricultural industry. the need for a basis for development. consideration of the environment, general nature protection interests and open-air interests. consideration of the settlement.’;» This involves making a number of discretionary judgements to adapt practices to changes in development and changing policy views. Over time, the needs of future generations will change. The agricultural industry as such is not static either, and among other things. housing considerations will also differ according to changing interests and wishes. One should expect the Minister of Agriculture and Food to continuously assess what is most beneficial to society in terms of ownership and use conditions and the protection of agricultural production areas. Section 9 of Chapter 4 of the Concession Act refers to five aspects of whether or not to grant a concession. These are: whether the agreed price corresponds to a socially sound price development as to the purpose of the transferee, taking into account the location in the area where the acquisition involves an operationally sound solution if the transferee is considered fit to operate the property if the acquisition takes into account the overall management of resources and the cultural landscape; As stated in the reference in Section 9 of the Concession Act, there are five different aspects to consider when considering an application for a concession. In the preparatory work of the law, it was said in its time that the list is not exhaustive, and that other elements can also be involved in the decision. The members of the Committee of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party consider it to be critical that the government sent letters to municipalities, county councillors and the State's agricultural administration to in effect disregard Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1 in the treatment of concession, because the government will propose to cancel this. In the recommendation for a decision, the Labour Party, the Centre Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party propose the following: ‘The Commission asks that a letter entitled ‘Practice of the price control of 4 December 2013 to the municipalities, the county councillors and the state agricultural management to disregard Section 9 of the First paragraph of the concession law. 1 immediately be withdrawn.’;» I must say I am surprised at the content of this proposal for the Stortinget. The Minister for Agriculture and Food has not said that one should disregard Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1st@item: inlistbox This isn't right. What the Council of State has said in its letter – and I find reason to refer it again – is: ‘The government wants to increase the turnover of agricultural properties and has the ambition to review legislation with a view to simplification. However, this could take some time. On this basis, the Ministry considers that price control practices should facilitate increased turnover. The Ministry therefore recommends that the price agreed by the parties be not given decisive weight in the assessment of whether a concession should be granted.’;» Unfortunately, with the proposal for a decision by the red-green parties referred to in I, it may seem that the Stortinget is led to believe that it has been asked by the department to disregard Section 9, first paragraph, No. 1st@item: inlistbox This is not the case. The Ministry has recommended that the price should not be given decisive weight in the assessment of whether to grant a concession. It's something completely different. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e1977	I would first of all like to thank the interpellant for asking this important question and thus helping to raise an important issue. Most of us live well, but too many people struggle to get a safe home. Without housing, it is difficult to move on, whether it is about education or work, and an unsafe living environment creates an uncertain framework around the growth of children. Therefore, housing-social work is a high priority area for government, and the work is part of our field of activity on a stronger social security network. In March, five government councils presented a national strategy for housing-social work – Housing for well-being. National strategy for housing social work [[2014-2020]] The aim is to bring together and target efforts towards disadvantaged people in the housing market. The strategy provides an overview of a complex policy area where responsibilities and tasks are shared between different sectors and levels of administration. The strategy clarifies responsibilities, tasks and expectations in the social housing work. The strategy is a long-term one and has to go to 2020. Those struggling in the housing market shall meet with respect and receive the necessary assistance. It is our responsibility to coordinate and coordinate the state efforts to build up under the local authorities' work in the social housing sector. As the interpellant refers to, people live most well in Norway today. The standard of living just gets better and better. Still, there's someone outside. The housing committee suggested that it was approx. 150000 disadvantaged on the housing market. The analysis shows that close to 60 pst. of the disadvantaged dwellings, compared to 18 pst. in the rest of the population. The proportion of people with disabilities is highest in Oslo. Immigrants are overrepresented among the disadvantaged in the housing market. A higher proportion of the disadvantaged have an unstable link to the labour market. The sample estimated that at the lower edge of 25 pst. of the disadvantaged households are families with children. While most higher-income households own their own dwelling, the proportion of owners with the lowest income is only 32 pst. For many, renting a dwelling may be the best option, but some households have problems getting a suitable rental dwelling. Few rental homes, low variation in the housing supply and discrimination between applicants for housing are among the challenges on the housing market. A 2012 survey shows that approx. 6,250 were homeless. The number is more or less unchanged from the 2008 mapping. However, the number of homeless children has increased, from 400 to 679. A little over half of the homeless depend on drugs, and two out of five have mental health challenges. Two out of five homeless people are registered in one of the four largest cities. On 1 January 2013, 3,829 refugees were accepted and awaited residence. By 31 March 2014, the figure was increased to 5,549. Lack of suitable housing is one of the reasons why municipalities cannot accept more refugees. I'm drawing these figures out because they show the complexity of the challenges we're facing, and the importance of implementing the strategy that we've now agreed on. There is no doubt that having a home — a home — creates security and a better opportunity to take education, work, set up a family and have a social life. Housing is often the prerequisite for participation in the other arenas of society. Without a good place to live, other challenges in life will be reinforced and go beyond the quality of life. Housing work is about everyone having the opportunity to master their everyday life – having a good housing and a manageable housing economy and being part of a community environment. No one shall be required to earn or qualify for assistance. Everyone has to live, and with the right help, everyone can live. The strategy has set three overall national targets for housing social work in the coming years. These are: Everyone should have a good place to live. All those in need of services shall be assisted in mastering the housing situation. Public efforts shall be comprehensive and effective. We strengthen efforts towards families with children and young people who do not live well. This is important for the children here and now and to prevent poor living conditions from inheriting. We have set out the following three concrete performance objectives in the strategy: Renting housing for families with children shall be of good quality and in a safe living environment. Temporary housing offers shall only be used exceptionally by families with children and young people, and such stays shall not exceed three months. Homelessness among families with children and young people shall be prevented and reduced. Municipalities have a key role and a primary responsibility to help disadvantaged people in the housing market. They shall contribute to the provision of housing to persons who are unable to take care of their own interests in the housing market. Many municipalities provide suitable housing for the disadvantaged. At state level, we can help to ensure good framework conditions for this work for municipalities. The Government has therefore proposed in the revised national budget for 2014 a strong increase in the commitment framework for rental properties. The increase implies that this year it can be facilitated for about a year. 1,500 new rental homes. This shows that the government is putting action behind words, and confirms that we want to strengthen the housing social work. Difficult housing is a diverse group of people with different challenges and needs. Some people need help with their bouts, others need help to get a suitable home after staying in a crisis centre, in an institution or in a prison. Some are in reception and are waiting for a municipality to settle in, while others need help to master the housing situation. The variety challenges the auxiliaries. We hear about unfortunate events that could have been avoided if we in the public sector had cooperated better. We need tools that are flexible and are designed to meet the challenges each individual has to face. The House Bank has been assigned the role of State coordinator to follow up the strategy. Six directorates have jointly been given the task of ensuring that words become action. The five others are the Directorate for Labour and Welfare, the Directorate for Children, Youth and Family Affairs, the Directorate for Health, the Directorate for Integration and Diversity and the Directorate for Criminal Welfare. Together with the municipalities, the Husbank is responsible for a number of important schemes aimed at helping people to find housing and to become residents: housing support, start-up loans, basic loans, start-up grants and rental housing. Just because there are so many different agencies that have a responsibility or a partial responsibility in this area, and because there are so many different schemes, coordination, coordination, is essential for us to succeed, both in order to have a coherent and coordinated state to deal with, and in order for municipalities to meet the individual with respect. In addition, the five other directorates manage mechanisms that are important for achieving the objectives of the strategy. Seeing the instruments on the housing and services side in the context is challenging, but, as I have stressed, very necessary. We have asked the Directorates to take action to target and coordinate state schemes. I am also pleased that the representative Mudasar Kapur addresses the relationship with non-governmental organisations and non-governmental and public agencies. I would like to stress that non-governmental organisations are key partners for the state and local authorities in the social work of housing. Several have a long tradition of cooperating with the public on housing social work. We also want to strengthen user involvement. Users, relatives and stakeholder organisations shall be involved in the design of residential social work. Today, we have a lot of knowledge about the challenges and solutions of housing social work. Many municipalities are well in the process of developing comprehensive and targeted social work. These are practices and experiences that must be shared with other municipalities. We want to cooperate with the municipalities, but also with the parties in Stortinget, to find the best solutions.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e2299	The Family and Culture Committee has had Meld. I would like to thank the Committee for the work that has been carried out, as rapporteur for the work that has been carried out. On 25 March of this year, the grant scheme was amended. There is now a dynamic ceiling and production grants can be provided for publication on all platforms – except broadcasting. The work on the conversion and modernisation of the scheme is thus well under way. Since its introduction in 1969, the device has been modified several times. The fact that it has now been made platform neutral is a further modernisation and adapts the scheme to a day-to-day media where changes take place very quickly. We're probably in the middle of the biggest revolution in the history of printed and written mass media. The ability of the public to exercise its freedom of expression, to publish its own opinions, is now almost innumerable. It has been so rapid and has been so cumbersome that it must be said that social media have revolutionized the access of the public to express itself freely. Blogs, web comments, new websites, Twitter messages, Facebook pages and Instagram images are published every second. This is just a tiny selection of all the possibilities that have followed and that continue to follow, in the wake of this development. In this picture, however, we still see an important communication task for news and timeliness media, as well as facilitating a broad public debate. But the editor's role today is a completely different one, with a completely different and less force than a few years ago. I don't think anyone – even the most balled editor – dares to predict what this looks like in a short time. It is therefore very important that we do not preserve a support scheme and hope for the best. We need to make a continuous assessment of the device and form of support. The ten largest newspapers receive almost 75 pst. of the 308 million. the amount of the aid. Over 110 other newspapers distribute the rest. These are small local guides that are at least equally important contributions to the diversity we want. Or if we rain a little further: the five largest receivers – the daily newspaper, our country, the class struggle, the nations and the day – receive 53 pst. of the money. To put it another way: 4 pst. the newspapers run off with more than half of the money. This distribution and the device may not be perfect. Therefore, following the majority view of the Committee, the scheme should be subject to further revision and modernisation – first and foremost through a static ceiling of 40 million. kr. This would with today's level given room to re-dispose $7 million. to small and important local authorities in the districts. In the position of the second largest beneficiary, Our Country, the editorial board appears more supportive than supported. When we argue that a crown cap will release funds for the smaller newspapers, it goes to the editor of our country, who points to parliamentary practice, instead of helping to discuss the support system. The production grant is a framework scheme. That means if someone gets more, others get less. This can be illustrated by the following example: the two largest recipients of press support, the Newspaper and Our Country – gathered in the company Mentor Media – receive nearly 30 p.m. of the whole pot. With a $40 million head. NOK 80 million would be spent on the two, which have good soundness and satisfactory profitability. kr instead of today's SEK 87 million. kr. Then other actors within the same scheme would receive 7 million. kr more, and there is a lot of money for many small and important local viewers. When we want to modernise the system, it's because we see that it's going wrong and diversity is being challenged. This shows why it is important to continue working on how this support is distributed. Society is changing rapidly – the media industry is changing rapidly. Norway’s very largest newspaper, VG, suggests that they are gone as paper newspapers in a few years’ time. This is a new reality. If we are to preserve diversity and ensure it, we can no longer doubt about conservation arrangements. This is what the majority of the Committee sees as continuing the need to modernise the instrument through increased non-discrimination and better targeting. So this is a continuous work in a constantly changing media landscape. I note that the Workers' Party has turned 180 degrees and left its previously well-founded position on a static roof – and is now adapting the reasoning accordingly. It is also disappointing to see that the Group of the Left has set aside the party programme, thereby also putting an end to a more modern and targeted system than we have today. This reverse operation could probably be noticed at locals who now get less than they could have. Together with the media, production aid constitutes the two most important contributions to a diverse press. Mediemomas and production support are also the respirator of the paper paper newspapers. I hope we can in the future find together and get a majority of modern solutions adapted to development. As it is now, we still go with our backs into the future and with our eyes firmly attached to the slowly dying paper newspapers.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e2374	The diversity of the media landscape is of great importance to Norwegian democracy. Free media and the right to speak through the written word are fundamental to any democratic society. News and timeliness media have a vital social function by targeting a variety of opinions, providing free and critical journalism and contributing to the debate on society. This is the starting point for media policy in Norway, and not least the starting point for today’s debate. The use of the Internet and social media has revolutionised the ability of the public to exercise its freedom of expression. While the new opportunities for expression constitute overall strength for democracy, news and timeliness media continue to have an important role to play in communicating and facilitating a broad public debate. The direct production grant scheme was established in 1969, with the aim of maintaining a differentiated daily press. The working party is pleased that the production grant is now being converted from a subsidy reserved for paper newspapers to a platform neutral grant. The daily media and media consumption of users are changing rapidly, and it is right to facilitate the transition to forward-looking news dissemination through a platform-neutral production grant. It is essential that the grant goes to media with a real need for support and contributes to a broad and nuanced public debate. The production grant has contributed to a wide variety of newspapers across the country, which has led to the Nordics being the world's most news-reading people. This is an ambition we must also have for the future – also on new platforms. The grant must be seen in conjunction with all media support schemes, including media SMEs. The production grant has two objectives: to contribute to a diversity of small localities, and to stimulate diversity of opinion in the major cities. The working party considers that there is a need to increase the production grant and, in this respect, refers to Prop. 1 S for 2013–2014, where the red-green government proposed to increase Chapter 335 entry 21 by 23,8 million. kr compared to 2103. In a joint comment, the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party warn the government against proposing cuts in the production grant, as was done in Prop. 1 S Appendix 1 for 2013–2014, as this will affect the smallest localities. The Labour Party is pleased with the work done by the Christian People's Party and the Left in the budget negotiations to ensure that there was no clean cut in the 2014 production grant. The proposals to either introduce a static aid ceiling of 40 million. kr or a dynamic roof of 40 pst. total operational costs have been consulted. Most hearing bodies are in favour of a system with a dynamic ceiling. Such a cap will allow for increased competition — including in the major cities — and contribute to increased public opinion. The government states that the production grant is a framework scheme, that it will provide more support to some actors, and that it means less support to others. I would point out that the level of appropriations is set annually, and the Workers' Party assumes that the facility and level of the production aid will take into account the real needs of the beneficiaries for financial support. We have noticed that the Department considers it impractical to abolish the dynamic grant package that has already been implemented in the scheme, which the Workers' Party agrees with. We assume that a dynamic aid ceiling related to the proportion of the operating costs of the beneficiaries was a necessary prerequisite for the ESA’s approval of the scheme. The Labour Party points out that in an industry which has so much of its income from the Community, the Community should also be able to impose conditions and restrictions on the withdrawal of dividends and options based on the public support schemes. We have noticed some media headlines about high management salaries and dividends, and believe that part of the industry itself has to bear responsibility for some of the crisis that many newspapers are experiencing today. The Workers' Party will therefore propose that the Government investigate how to ensure that any future increases in support schemes are protected and used for the purpose, which is journalism and dissatisfaction. On behalf of the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party, I address the proposal referred to in the vote: ‘The Government is asked not to introduce a static ceiling on production aid.’ In addition, the Workers' Party will make the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to investigate how to ensure that any future increases in support schemes are used for the purpose of journalism and diversity of opinion.’

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-19.ana.ud2279e2783	The media industry is in a demanding transition phase. Both the printing and advertising revenue for the paper newspapers falls dramatically and much faster than expected. The situation clearly gives cause for concern. The media industry needs to find its place in the new digital economy, find business models that can complement and eventually replace those that the newspapers have been based on for 150 years. I believe that Norwegian media houses have a unique opportunity to succeed in this. Few other countries have such a highly educated population, with a good economy, well-developed infrastructure and a great willingness to use new media services. The main government can therefore do so by providing industry with framework conditions and space for action for development and innovation. It is the industry itself that has to define its place in the new media economy, and the government cannot. However, the government will facilitate this, for example: by ensuring that public instruments are effective, targeted and non-discriminatory publishing platforms. We have therefore introduced the production grant for news and news media with effect from this year. The new scheme is platform neutral. That is, grants no longer depend on, or are intended solely on the basis of, paper publication. This is a very important change, because we now have an aid scheme that leaves it to the industry itself to decide how, and on which technological platform, the content is to be disseminated. We believe this will promote innovation and development and help to help the media industry through the digital transition. We see that there are still new trends in the media industry, and on Saturday we could read about a new bloom of digging journalists online in the United States. This trend has so far led to 5,000 new full-time jobs in different websites. A member of the board of Facebook and eBay leads to a new golden age for journalism, with a growth of between 10 and 100 times in the years to come: Maybe we've gone into a new golden age for journalism, we just haven't recognized it yet, writes Marc Andreessen on his website. One of the main aims of the production grant is to promote media pluralism. We therefore have a responsibility to ensure that the funds allocated to the scheme are used effectively. This can initially be challenging because the production grant is a framework scheme. If a large part of the framework is allocated to one or a few actors, there may be little left to the others. This does not promote diversity. Many people assume that the funds are spread, so that there are many different votes. In line with the proposal of the Government of Stoltenberg II, we have therefore proposed in the Storing Communication that an absolute grant cap of 40 million be introduced. kr per media company per year from 2015 onwards. The previous government has already notified a static grant cap to ESA in preparation for deployment. I would like to stress that the proposed 40 million cap. does not involve a cut in the overall framework of the press support. The funds released by individual actors hit the 40 million ceiling. kr, will not leave the scheme, but will be used for redistribution from a few to a large majority of recipients. Finally, I would like to recall that the proposal to set a ceiling of 40 million. kr is a continuation of a proposal from the previous government. In June last year, the government of Stoltenberg II notified ESA that the scheme would enter into force with a grant cap of 40 million. kr. After a hearing, they chose to maintain this proposal. That's what we've been doing. There are good reasons why not a few individual media should receive almost a third of the press support. I have noticed that the members of the Committee from the Workers' Party have now turned around in this matter, by the fact that these now go against a static ceiling. I think this is unfortunate, because we are at risk of individual actors getting a disproportionate share of the media support, with the result that we are getting less diversity for the money. I am otherwise glad that we now, with unanimous support in Stortinget, also get platform neutrality in terms of the production grant – that one is like either a conveyer of his message on paper or digitally.
